Adaptive exact recovery in sparse nonparametric models
Authors: Natalia Stepanova, Marie Turcicova
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
We observe an unknown function of d variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\textbf{t})$$\end{document}f(t), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{t}\in [0,1]^d$$\end{document}t∈[0,1]d, in the Gaussian white noise model of intensity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon >0$$\end{document}ε>0. We assume that the function f is regular and that it is a sum of k-variate functions, where k varies from 1 to s (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le s\le d$$\end{document}1≤s≤d). These functions are unknown to us and only a few of them are nonzero. In this article, we address the problem of identifying the nonzero components of f in the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=d_\varepsilon \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}d=dε→∞ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0 and s is either fixed or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=s_\varepsilon \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}s=sε→∞, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=o(d)$$\end{document}s=o(d) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}ε→∞. This may be viewed as a variable selection problem. We derive the conditions when exact variable selection in the model at hand is possible and provide a selection procedure that achieves this type of selection. The procedure is adaptive to a degree of model sparsity described by the sparsity parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in (0,1)$$\end{document}β∈(0,1). We also derive conditions that make the exact variable selection impossible. Our results augment previous work in this area.

Introduction

Analysis of high-dimensional data has recently become more and more important since large datasets have become increasingly available in every field of research – ranging from genomic sequencing and brain imaging to climate monitoring and social sciences. However, such analysis is often associated with specific phenomena that go beyond classical statistical theory. The main difficulty is that the dimension can be very large. Speaking in mathematical terms, it is natural to assume that the dimension tends to infinity.

In this work, we address the problem of adaptive recovery of the sparsity pattern of a multivariate signal observed in the Gaussian white noise, and augment the results obtained earlier in Ingster and Stepanova (2014) and Stepanova and Turcicova (2025). Specifically, we assume that an unknown signal f of d variables is observed in the Gaussian white noise model1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} dX_\varepsilon (\textbf{t})=f(\textbf{t})d\textbf{t}+\varepsilon d W(\textbf{t}),\quad \textbf{t}\in [0,1]^d, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dXε(t)=f(t)dt+εdW(t),t∈[0,1]d,where dW is a d-parameter Gaussian white noise and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon >0$$\end{document}ε>0 is the noise intensity. The signal f belongs to a subspace of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_2([0,1]^d)=L_2^d$$\end{document}L2([0,1]d)=L2d with inner product \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\cdot ,\cdot )_2$$\end{document}(·,·)2 and norm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _2$$\end{document}‖·‖2 that consists of regular enough functions, and we assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=d_\varepsilon \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}d=dε→∞ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0. Consider an operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {W}:L_2^d\rightarrow \mathcal{G}_0$$\end{document}W:L2d→G0 taking values in the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{G}_0$$\end{document}G0 of centered Gaussian random variables such that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi _0=\mathbb {W}(g_1)$$\end{document}ξ0=W(g1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _0=\mathbb {W}(g_2)$$\end{document}η0=W(g2), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1,g_2\in L_2^d$$\end{document}g1,g2∈L2d, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{cov}(\xi _0,\eta _0)=(g_1,g_2)_{2}$$\end{document}cov(ξ0,η0)=(g1,g2)2. In addition to regularity constraints, we assume that f has a sparse structure and consider the problem of recovering exactly the sparsity pattern of f by using the asymptotically minimax approach.

Sparsity conditions

In high-dimensional inference problems like the one under study, the quality of statistical procedures deteriorates very quickly as the dimension increases. One way to avoid the curse of dimensionality stemming from high-dimensional settings is to reduce the “working dimension” of the problem. In the present setup, this can be done by employing functional ANOVA-type decompositions for f. One common functional ANOVA model (see, for example, Lin (2000), Ingster and Suslina (2015)) assumes that the function of d variables is a sum of functions of one variable (main effects), functions of two variables (two-way interactions), and so on. That is, the function f of d variables is decomposed as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} f(\textbf{t})&=\sum _{u\subseteq \{1,\ldots ,d\}} f_u(\textbf{t}_u),\quad \textbf{t}=(t_1,\ldots ,t_d)\in [0,1]^d, \quad \textbf{t}_{u}=(t_j)_{j \in u}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(t)=∑u⊆{1,…,d}fu(tu),t=(t1,…,td)∈[0,1]d,tu=(tj)j∈u,where the sum is taken over all subsets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \subseteq \{1,\ldots ,d\}$$\end{document}u⊆{1,…,d}. Owen (1998) noted that the functional ANOVA decomposition is completely analogous to the one used in experimental statistics because the total variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^2 = \int _{[0,1]^d} (f(\textbf{t}) - {\mathbb {I}})^2 d\textbf{t}$$\end{document}σ2=∫[0,1]d(f(t)-I)2dt, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {I}} = \int _{[0,1]^d} f(\textbf{t}) d\textbf{t}$$\end{document}I=∫[0,1]df(t)dt, can be written as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^2 = \sum _{u\subseteq \{1,\ldots ,d\}} \sigma ^2_u$$\end{document}σ2=∑u⊆{1,…,d}σu2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _u^2 = \int _{[0,1]^k} f_u^2(\textbf{t}_u) d \textbf{t}_u$$\end{document}σu2=∫[0,1]kfu2(tu)dtu if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \ne \emptyset $$\end{document}u≠∅ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^2_{\emptyset } = 0$$\end{document}σ∅2=0. In Ingster and Suslina (2015), this decomposition is used to set up nonparametric alternatives to the hypothesis of no signal in a signal detection problem. In Owen (1998) and Lin (2000), the functional ANOVA decomposition is used to split the problem of integrating and estimating a multivariate function f into low-dimensional tasks.

It is assumed that, in the above functional ANOVA decomposition, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_u=\textrm{const}$$\end{document}fu=const for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=\emptyset $$\end{document}u=∅ and, in order to guarantee uniqueness, that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{0}^1 f_u(\textbf{t}_u)\,dt_j=0,\quad \text{ for }\; j\in u, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫01fu(tu)dtj=0,forj∈u,that is, the terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_u$$\end{document}fu are mutually orthogonal in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_2^d$$\end{document}L2d. Each function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_u(\textbf{t}_u)$$\end{document}fu(tu) depends only on variables in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{t}_u$$\end{document}tu and describes the “interaction” between these variables. Denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\#(u)$$\end{document}#(u) the number of elements of a set u and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{t}_{-u} = (t_j)_{j \notin u}$$\end{document}t-u=(tj)j∉u. One example of the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_u(\textbf{t}_u)$$\end{document}fu(tu) that satisfies the above orthogonality condition is (see Owen (1998) for details)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_u (\textbf{t}_u) = \int _{[0,1]^{d-\#(u)}} \left( f(\textbf{t}) - \sum _{\begin{array}{c} v \subset u \\ v\ne u \end{array}} f_v(\textbf{t}_v)\right) d\textbf{t}_{-u}.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}fu(tu)=∫[0,1]d-#(u)f(t)-∑v⊂uv≠ufv(tv)dt-u.Another example is the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_u(\textbf{t}_u)=\prod _{j\in u}f_j(t_j)$$\end{document}fu(tu)=∏j∈ufj(tj), where each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_j$$\end{document}fj integrates to zero.

In high-dimensional inference problems, alternatively, or in addition to the general functional ANOVA model, we may choose some s (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le s\le d$$\end{document}1≤s≤d) and consider the expansion\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\textbf{t})=\sum _{ \begin{array}{c} u\subseteq \{1,\ldots ,d\},\, 1\le \# (u) \le s \end{array}} f_u(\textbf{t}_u),\quad \textbf{t}\in [0,1]^d, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(t)=∑u⊆{1,…,d},1≤#(u)≤sfu(tu),t∈[0,1]d,where, if s is small relative to d, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\textbf{t})$$\end{document}f(t) is a sum of functions of a small number of variables. The model of interest in this work is obtained from the above orthogonal expansion by assuming additionally that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\textbf{t})$$\end{document}f(t) has a sparse structure. To ensure that the recovery of the d-variate signal f in model (2) is feasible, the sparsity and regularity constraints on f are required.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le s$$\end{document}1≤k≤s, where s is as above, define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{U}_{k,d}=\{u_k:u_k\subseteq \{1,\ldots ,d\}, $$\end{document}Uk,d={uk:uk⊆{1,…,d}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \#(u_k)=k\}$$\end{document}#(uk)=k} and observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\# (\mathcal {U}_{k,d})={d\atopwithdelims ()k}.$$\end{document}#(Uk,d)=dk. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} sparsity index. Condition (5) is the sparsity condition that determines the sparsity structure of f in (3). This condition implies that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k<l\le s$$\end{document}1≤k<l≤s, the number of k-variate terms on the right-hand side of (3) that are active is less than the number of l-variate terms that are active.

In a simpler setup, when instead of decomposition (3) in model (2)–(5), the signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} identifying exactly the active components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} In these articles, conditions under which exact identification of the nonzero components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\textbf{t})$$\end{document}f(t) as in (6) is possible and impossible were established, and adaptive selection procedures that, under some model assumptions, identify exactly all nonzero k-variate components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{u_k}$$\end{document}fuk were proposed.

In this work, we consider the two cases: (i) when s is fixed and (ii) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=s_\varepsilon \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}s=sε→∞, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=o(d)$$\end{document}s=o(d) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0. In both cases, the sparsity condition (5) yields\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{k=1}^{s} \sum _{u_k \in \mathcal {U}_{k,d}} \eta _{u_k}= \sum _{k=1}^s {d\atopwithdelims ()k}^{1-\beta }(1+o(1))= {d\atopwithdelims ()s}^{1-\beta }(1+o(1)), \quad \varepsilon \rightarrow 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑k=1s∑uk∈Uk,dηuk=∑k=1sdk1-β(1+o(1))=ds1-β(1+o(1)),ε→0,that is, only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d\atopwithdelims ()s}^{1-\beta }(1+o(1))=o({d\atopwithdelims ()s})$$\end{document}ds1-β(1+o(1))=o(ds) orthogonal components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{u_k}$$\end{document}fuk of f in (3) are active and the remaining components are inactive, which implies that the function f is sparse. The values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β that are close to 1 make the signal f in (3) highly sparse, whereas the values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β that are close to 0 make it dense. Condition (5), in which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le s\le d$$\end{document}1≤s≤d, may be viewed as a natural extension of the sparsity condition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{u_k\in \mathcal{U}_{k,d}}\eta _{u_k}= {d\atopwithdelims ()k}^{1-\beta }(1+o(1))\quad \varepsilon \rightarrow 0,\quad \text{ for } \text{ some }\;1\le k\le d, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑uk∈Uk,dηuk=dk1-β(1+o(1))ε→0,forsome1≤k≤d,employed in Stepanova and Turcicova (2025) to recover the sparsity pattern of function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\textbf{t})$$\end{document}f(t) as in (6) for a chosen k.

Define the sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${H}_{\beta ,d}^k={H}_{\beta ,d}^k(\varepsilon )$$\end{document}Hβ,dk=Hβ,dk(ε), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le s$$\end{document}1≤k≤s, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^{s}_{\beta ,d} =\mathcal {H}^{s}_{\beta ,d}(\varepsilon )$$\end{document}Hβ,ds=Hβ,ds(ε) as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {H}_{\beta ,d}^k&= \left\{ \varvec{\eta }_k=( \eta _{u_k}, u_k \in \mathcal {U}_{k,d}) : \eta _{u_k} \in \{0,1\},\, u_k\in \mathcal{U}_{k,d},\; This may be viewed as a variable selection problem, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\varvec{\eta }}}$$\end{document}η^ may be named a selector.

Regularity conditions

Attempting to provide an asymptotically minimax solution to the problem of recovering the sparsity pattern of a function f in model (2)–(5), we have to assume that the set of signals f is not “too large". In this article, we will be interested in periodic Sobolev classes described by means of Fourier coefficients. Such classes are quite common in the literature on nonparametric estimation, signal detection, and variable selection.

Namely, following the construction in Ingster and Suslina (2015), for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Problem statement

Based on the “observation” \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {X}_\varepsilon $$\end{document}Xε in model (2)–(5) and (12), we wish to identify, with a high degree of accuracy, the active components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{u_k}$$\end{document}fuk of f. This leads to the problem of obtaining an estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}=(\varvec{\hat{\eta }}_1,\ldots , \varvec{\hat{\eta }}_s)$$\end{document}η^=(η^1,…,η^s) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\eta }}=({\varvec{\eta }}_1,\ldots ,{\varvec{\eta }}_s)\in \mathcal {H}^s_{\beta ,d}$$\end{document}η=(η1,…,ηs)∈Hβ,ds, which may be viewed as a variable selection problem, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}$$\end{document}η^ selects important variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_j, \, j=1,\ldots ,d$$\end{document}tj,j=1,…,d, and their relevant interactions in the ANOVA-type expansion (3). The estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}$$\end{document}η^ is named a selector since it serves to select the active components of f in decomposition (3).

Identifying the active components of f in model (2)–(5) and (12) is feasible when the components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{u_k}$$\end{document}fuk of f are not “too small”, i.e., they are separated from zero in an appropriate way. Therefore, for a given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} To ensure that exact recovery of the sparsity pattern of f (in the sense defined precisely at the end of this section) is possible, we require the components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{u_k}$$\end{document}fuk, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_k\in \mathcal{U}_{k,d}$$\end{document}uk∈Uk,d, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le s$$\end{document}1≤k≤s, to be selectable, that is, we require the family (indexed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon $$\end{document}ε) of collections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_\varepsilon =\{r_{\varepsilon ,k}, 1\le k\le s\}$$\end{document}rε={rε,k,1≤k≤s} in the hypothesis testing problem (13) to be above the so-called sharp selection boundary. Establishing the sharp selection boundary that makes the exact recovery of f in model (2)–(5) and (12) feasible is one of the goals in this article. For this purpose, the hypothesis testing problem (13) will be employed as an auxiliary problem.

A related measure of risk that is often used in the literature on variable selection is the probability of wrong recovery, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {P}_{\varepsilon ,f}(\varvec{\hat{\eta }}\ne {\varvec{\eta }})$$\end{document}Pε,f(η^≠η), that determines the chance that a selector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}$$\end{document}η^ will not agree with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\eta }}$$\end{document}η. Most literature on variable selection in high dimensions focuses on constructing selectors such that the probability of wrong recovery is close to zero in some asymptotic sense (see, for example, Wainwright (2009), Wasserman and Roeder (2009), Zhang (2010), Zhao and Yu (2006)). Although the probability of wrong recovery has been used extensively by other authors, the Hamming risk is a more general measure of risk since, by means of Markov’s inequality,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \operatorname {P}_{\varepsilon ,f}(\varvec{\hat{\eta }}\ne {\varvec{\eta }})=\operatorname {P}_{\varepsilon ,f}(|\varvec{\hat{\eta }}-\varvec{\eta }|\ge 1) \le {\operatorname {E}}_{\varepsilon ,f} | \varvec{\hat{\eta }}-\varvec{\eta } |. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pε,f(η^≠η)=Pε,f(|η^-η|≥1)≤Eε,f|η^-η|.In view of the last inequality, if the Hamming risk tends to zero, the probability of wrong recovery is guaranteed to tend to zero. For this reason, we only consider the Hamming risk as a measure of selector performance.

Sparse Gaussian sequence space model

We shall study the recovery problem at hand in the sequence space of Fourier coefficients of f and name it the variable selection problem. A Gaussian sequence space model is equivalent to the corresponding Gaussian white noise model but is more convenient to deal with since it is written in terms of the Fourier coefficients.

Summary of the main results

Consider the problem of exact identification of the nonzero index variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _{u_k}$$\end{document}ηuk in the sequence space model (16), and recall the family of collections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_\varepsilon =\{r_{\varepsilon ,k},1\le k\le s\}$$\end{document}rε={rε,k,1≤k≤s} that determines the sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathring{\Theta }_{\textbf{c}_{u_k}}(r_{\varepsilon ,k}) $$\end{document}Θ˚cuk(rε,k), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_k\in \mathcal{U}_{k,d}$$\end{document}uk∈Uk,d, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le s$$\end{document}1≤k≤s, as defined in (17). The main contribution of this work is the derivation of the sharp selection boundary, stated in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_\varepsilon =\{r_{\varepsilon ,k},1\le k\le s\}$$\end{document}rε={rε,k,1≤k≤s}, which defines when exact selection of the nonzero index variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _{u_k}$$\end{document}ηuk in model (16) is possible and when this type of selection is impossible. This boundary is determined by inequalities (36) and (40) bellow. Then, we construct an adaptive (independent of the sparsity index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β) estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} }}$$\end{document}η^ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\eta }}\in \mathcal{H}^{s}_{\beta ,d}$$\end{document}η∈Hβ,ds attaining this boundary with the _{s,d}^{\sigma }(r_{\varepsilon })} \operatorname {E}_{{\varvec{\theta }},{\varvec{\eta }}} | \varvec{\hat{\eta }}-\varvec{\eta }| = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}lim supε→0supη∈Hβ,dssupθ∈Θs,dσ(rε)Eθ,η|η^-η|=0,which holds for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} We also show that if the family of collections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} This implies that the proposed selector is optimal (in the minimax hypothesis testing sense).

The limiting relations (19) and (20) will be referred to as the upper bound on the maximum Hamming risk of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}$$\end{document}η^ and the lower bound on the minimax Hamming risk, respectively.

Previous results

In order to better situate our work in current literature, consider the problem of variable selection in high-dimensional regression. This problem, in its simplest form, consists of identifying active (nonzero) coordinates of a nonrandom vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{x}=(x_1,\ldots ,x_m)^{\top } \in \mathbb {R}^m$$\end{document}x=(x1,…,xm)⊤∈Rm observed with additive noise:21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{Y} = A \varvec{x} + \varvec{Z}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Y=Ax+Z,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{Y}=(Y_1,\ldots ,Y_n)^{\top }$$\end{document}Y=(Y1,…,Yn)⊤ is a response vector, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{Z}=(Z_1,\ldots ,Z_n)^{\top }$$\end{document}Z=(Z1,…,Zn)⊤ is a zero mean noise vector with some (usually diagonal) covariance matrix, A is an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\times m$$\end{document}n×m design matrix that is either deterministic (and often equal to identity) or has some known distribution, and the dimension m is much larger than the sample size n. To ensure that the recovery of the “signal” vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{x}$$\end{document}x in model (21) is feasible, the vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{x}$$\end{document}x is assumed to be sparse. Depending on the context, the recovery of the sparsity pattern of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{x}$$\end{document}x in model (21) is termed in the literature as variable selection, subset selection, support recovery, or multiple testing (see, for example, Wasserman and Roeder (2009), Belitser and Nurushev (2018), Butucea et al. (2018), Gao and Stove (2020), and references wherein). However, the goal is always the same – to correctly detect the support \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{S} = \{j: x_j \ne 0 \}$$\end{document}S={j:xj≠0} of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{x}$$\end{document}x. In addition to finding an estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{S}}_n(\varvec{Y})$$\end{document}S^n(Y) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{S}$$\end{document}S that recovers the positions of nonzero entries of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{x}$$\end{document}x, which is often called a selector or decoder of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{S}$$\end{document}S, it is also important to establish the minimal conditions under which the support set can be effectively estimated. The quality of an estimator of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{S}$$\end{document}S can be measured by various metrics, including the Hamming loss, defined as the number of mismatches between the estimated and true support set, the probability of support recovery \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {P}(\varvec{\hat{S}}_n(\varvec{Y}) = \varvec{S})$$\end{document}P(S^n(Y)=S), or the misdetection probability, defined as the probability that the number of misdetected components equals zero. The literature on support recovery in the high-dimensional linear model (21) under sparsity assumption is very rich and its complete overview would fall far beyond the format of this paper. So, we provide only an outline of some results that are relevant to our work.

Two most common approaches to variable selection are via penalization and thresholding. Penalization methods are suitable for situations where the dimension m is not very large and their core is imposing a proper penalty on the number of selected variables. The penalty can be given simply by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_1$$\end{document}l1-norm of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{x}$$\end{document}x or based on some information criterion (e.g., AIC, BIC) or even more complex (e.g., concave penalty as in Zhang (2010)). One of the most popular penalization methods, Lasso, uses the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_1$$\end{document}l1-norm of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{x}$$\end{document}x as a penalty term (see, for example, Zhao and Yu (2006), Wainwright (2009), Wasserman and Roeder (2009)). Lasso is efficient for relatively large m, but suffers from nonnegligible bias. For very large m, penalization becomes computationally demanding and methods based on univariate thresholding, also called marginal regression, come into play (see Genovese et al. (2012) and Ndaoud (2020)). An estimator of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{S}$$\end{document}S provided by a thresholding method has a simple form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{S}}_n (\varvec{Y})= \{j: |Y_j| > t(\varvec{Y})\}$$\end{document}S^n(Y)={j:|Yj|>t(Y)} and, with a suitable choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t(\varvec{Y})$$\end{document}t(Y), it is very fast and efficient. The threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t(\varvec{Y})$$\end{document}t(Y) can be set up in many different ways and in case of some iterative methods, it can be even updated at each step (see Ndaoud (2020)). The selection procedure introduced in this work also belongs to the class of thresholding methods.

The problem of variable selection in sparse nonparametric regression is far more challenging as compared to its parametric analogue. Therefore, the related results obtained thus far are not as numerous and diverse as those obtained in parametric settings. In a simpler setup, when instead of decomposition (3) in model (2)–(5) and (12) the signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\textbf{t})$$\end{document}f(t) is assumed to be a sum of functions each depending on k variables (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le d$$\end{document}1≤k≤d) as in (6), the problem of identifying the active components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\textbf{t})$$\end{document}f(t) was studied in Ingster and Stepanova (2014) and Butucea and Stepanova (2017) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1$$\end{document}k=1, and in Stepanova and Turcicova (2025) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le d$$\end{document}1≤k≤d. In these articles, conditions under which exact and almost full identification of the nonzero components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\textbf{t})$$\end{document}f(t) as in (6) is possible and impossible were established, and adaptive selection procedures that, under some model assumptions, identify exactly and almost fully all nonzero k-variate components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{u_k}$$\end{document}fuk were proposed. For more details, we refer to the discussion of Section 4.

Construction of adaptive selector

The idea behind the aggregate selector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}=(\varvec{\hat{\eta }}_k=(\hat{\eta }_{u_k},u_k \in \mathcal{U}_{k,d}),1\le k\le s)$$\end{document}η^=(η^k=(η^uk,uk∈Uk,d),1≤k≤s) defined for fixed s by (33) and (34) and for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=s_\varepsilon \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}s=sε→∞ by (33) and (35) is a natural one. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_k\in \mathcal{U}_{k,d}$$\end{document}uk∈Uk,d, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le s$$\end{document}1≤k≤s, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\eta }_{u_k}$$\end{document}η^uk identifies the component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{u_k}$$\end{document}fuk as active, when at least one of the statistics \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ S_{u_k,m}$$\end{document}Suk,m, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=1,\ldots ,M_k$$\end{document}m=1,…,Mk, detects it. Therefore, the probability of falsely non-identifying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} unknown) value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} For more details, see the proof of Theorem 1 in Section 3.1.

Main results

We first state the conditions when exact variable selection in model (16) is possible and show that the proposed selector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}=(\varvec{\hat{\eta }}_k=(\hat{\eta }_{u_k},u_k \in \mathcal{U}_{k,d}),1\le k\le s)$$\end{document}η^=(η^k=(η^uk,uk∈Uk,d),1≤k≤s) achieves this type of selection. Then, we demonstrate that our selector is the best possible in the asymptotically minimax sense. In the statements of Theorems 1 to 4 below, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_k$$\end{document}uk is an arbitrary element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{U}_{k,d}$$\end{document}Uk,d for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le s$$\end{document}1≤k≤s.

Upper bound on the maximum risk of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}$$\end{document}η^ when s is fixed and when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}s→∞

The next result states that if the elements of the family of collections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_\varepsilon =\{r_{\varepsilon ,k},1\le k\le s\}$$\end{document}rε={rε,k,1≤k≤s} in the hypothesis testing problem (18) all exceed a certain level, then the upper bound on the maximum Hamming risk of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}$$\end{document}η^ as defined in (19) holds true, and hence the proposed selector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}$$\end{document}η^ is exact.

Theorem 1 can be extended to the case of growing s.

Theorem 2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Proof

The proof goes in part along the same lines as that of Theorem 1, with condition (35) used instead of condition (34). Observe that, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}d→∞, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \begin{document}$$s=o(d)$$\end{document}s=o(d) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} The importance of condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\log \log d=o(s)$$\end{document}loglogd=o(s) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=s_\varepsilon \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}s=sε→∞, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=o(d)$$\end{document}s=o(d) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0 can be seen from the proof of Theorem 3.3 in Stepanova and Turcicova (2025). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Theorems 1 and 2 show that if a positive family of collections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_\varepsilon =\{r_{\varepsilon ,k}:1\le k\le s\}$$\end{document}rε={rε,k:1≤k≤s} is such that condition (36) holds, exact variable selection in the sequence space model (16) is possible; this is true for fixed s as well as for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=s_\varepsilon \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}s=sε→∞, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=o(d)$$\end{document}s=o(d) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0. Theorems 1 and 2 may be viewed as extensions of Theorems 3.1 and 3.3 in Stepanova and Turcicova (2025), for more details we refer to the discussion of Section 4.

Lower bound on the minimax risk when s is fixed and when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}s→∞

We now turn to deriving the conditions when exact variable selection in model (16) is impossible, i.e. when the lower bound (20) holds true, which occurs if the family of collections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_\varepsilon =\{r_{\varepsilon ,k},1\le k\le s\}$$\end{document}rε={rε,k,1≤k≤s} in the hypothesis testing problem (18) falls below a certain level. The precise statement is as follows.

\end{aligned}$$\end{document}lim infε→0infη~supη∈Hβ,dssupθ∈Θs,dσ(rε)Eθ,η|η~-η|>0,where the infimum is taken over all selectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\eta }}\in \mathcal {H}^s_{\beta ,d}$$\end{document}η∈Hβ,ds in model (16).

Proof

The result of Theorem 3 can be extended to the case of growing s.

Theorem 4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Proof

Using the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem 3, with reference to Theorem 3.4 of Stepanova and Turcicova (2025) instead of reference to Theorem 3.2 of Stepanova and Turcicova (2025), we arrive at the statement of Theorem 4. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Theorems 3 and 4 show that if a positive family of collections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_\varepsilon =\{r_{\varepsilon ,k}:1\le k\le s\}$$\end{document}rε={rε,k:1≤k≤s} is such that condition (40) holds, exact variable selection in the sequence space model (16) is impossible; this is true for fixed s as well as for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=s_\varepsilon \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}s=sε→∞ \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=o(d)$$\end{document}s=o(d) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0.Theorems 3 and 4 may be viewed as extensions of Theorems 3.2 and 3.4 in Stepanova and Turcicova (2025), for more details we refer to the discussion in Section 4.

Discussion

By using the notion of optimality of a statistical procedure from the minimax hypothesis testing theory, we arrive at the following important conclusions. When s is fixed, in view of Theorems 1 and 3, the selector defined in (33) and (34) is seen to perform optimally with respect to a Hamming loss in the asymptotically minimax sense. Similarly, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=s_\varepsilon \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}s=sε→∞, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=o(d)$$\end{document}s=o(d) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0, by means of Theorems 2 and 4, the selector defined in (33) and (35) is optimal with respect to a Hamming loss in the asymptotically minimax sense.

We now explain our choice to state the sharp selection boundary in terms of the quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{\varepsilon ,u_k}(r_{\varepsilon ,k})$$\end{document}aε,uk(rε,k), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le s$$\end{document}1≤k≤s, rather than in terms of the radii \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{\varepsilon ,k}$$\end{document}rε,k, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le s$$\end{document}1≤k≤s. To this end, consider the Gaussian vector model42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X_{u_k}=\mu _{k,d}\eta _{u_k}+\varepsilon _{u_k},\quad u_k\in \mathcal{U}_{k,d},\quad 1\le k\le s, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Xuk=μk,dηuk+εuk,uk∈Uk,d,1≤k≤s,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{k,d}>0$$\end{document}μk,d>0 is the signal, the errors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _{u_k}$$\end{document}εuk are iid standard normal random variables, the deterministic quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _{u_k}\in \{0,1\}$$\end{document}ηuk∈{0,1} are as before and, in particular, satisfy the sparsity condition (5). For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _{u_k}=1$$\end{document}ηuk=1, the observation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{u_k}$$\end{document}Xuk is equal to the signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{k,d}$$\end{document}μk,d perturbed by random noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _{u_k}$$\end{document}εuk. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _{u_k}=0$$\end{document}ηuk=0, the observation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{u_k}$$\end{document}Xuk is merely random noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _{u_k}$$\end{document}εuk. Additionally, the use of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{\varepsilon ,u_k}(r_{\varepsilon ,k})$$\end{document}aε,uk(rε,k), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le s$$\end{document}1≤k≤s, for establishing the sharp selection boundary is natural for comparison purposes, as seen from the discussion below.

\usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{H}_{1,s}^{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}H1,sε), whereas, when the second inequality in (43) holds, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}_{0,s}$$\end{document}H0,s and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{H}_{1,s}^{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}H1,sε merge asymptotically (i.e., there is no consistent test procedure for testing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}_{0,s}$$\end{document}H0,s versus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textbf{H}_{1,s}^{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}H1,sε). By comparing the inequalities in (43) to the inequalities (36) and (40), we notice that the sharp selection boundary lies above the sharp detection boundary. This is not unusual because the problem of selecting nonzero components is more difficult than that of testing whether such nonzero components exit. In particular, in order to be selectable, the components need to be detectable.

Simulation study

To showcase the exact selection procedure based on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}=(\varvec{\hat{\eta }}_k=(\hat{\eta }_{u_k},u_k \in \mathcal{U}_{k,d}),1\le k\le s)$$\end{document}η^=(η^k=(η^uk,uk∈Uk,d),1≤k≤s) in (33)–(34) in its capacity to recover the sparsity pattern of a signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in \mathcal{F}^{\beta ,\sigma }_{s,d}(r_{\varepsilon })$$\end{document}f∈Fs,dβ,σ(rε) of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=\sum _{k=1}^s \sum _{u_k\in \mathcal{U}_{k,d}}\eta _{u_k} f_{u_k}$$\end{document}f=∑k=1s∑uk∈Uk,dηukfuk, we conduct a small-scale simulation study. The values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Err}(\varvec{\hat{\eta }})$$\end{document}Err(η^) for different d are seen in Table 2 in the last column corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =1$$\end{document}α=1, and they are all zero.

To illustrate the impact of a signal strength on the Hamming risk of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\hat{\eta }}$$\end{document}η^, we multiply the active component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{u_{1}^{(1)}}$$\end{document}fu1(1) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (0,1] $$\end{document}α∈(0,1], while keeping the other active components unchanged.
